Elements of $A$ are equivalence classes of Cauchy sequences $(\alpha_i)_{i=1}^\infty$. Let $\gamma_i = v_0(\alpha_i)$. If $\lim_{i\to\infty}\gamma_i=0$, then we put $v(a) = 0$. If $(\widetilde{\alpha}_i)$ is equivalent to the given one and $\delta\in\Gamma$, then $K = K_{v_0<\delta}$ is open in $\mathcal{A}$, hence there is $i$ such that $\alpha_i-\widetilde{\alpha}_i\in K$. Then $v_0(\widetilde{\alpha}_i)<\delta$ provided that $v_0(\alpha_i)<\delta$ which happens for sufficiently large $i$.

    % \par if $\lim_{i\to\infty}\gamma_i=0$ does not hold, there is $\delta\in\Gamma$ such that $\gamma_i\geq\delta$ for sufficiently large $i$. Since $K=K_{v_0<\delta}$ is open and $(\alpha_i)$ is a Cauchy sequence, there is $i'$ such that $\alpha_j-\alpha_k\in K$ when $(j,k)\geq i'$. By what was said before, there is $i>i'$ such that $\gamma_i\geq \delta$. If $j\geq i$, then $\alpha_i-\alpha_j\in K$ hence $\gamma_j=u_0(\alpha_j)=u_0(\alpha_i) = \gamma-i$ as $\alpha_i-\alpha_j\in K$. Thus $(\gamma_i)$ stabilizes to some fixed $\gamma\in \Gamma$ as $j\to\infty$, and we put $v(a) = \gamma$. If $\widetilde{\alpha}\sim\alpha$ then $\widetilde{\alpha}_j - \alpha_j\in K$ for $j>i''$ hence $u_0(\widetilde{\alpha}_j)=\gamma$ when $j>\max\{i,i''\}$. hence this case too is independent of choice of $\alpha$. One easily checks the rest.
\end{proof}
% 7/10
\begin{theorem}
    \label{theorem_21_1}
    In the situation of Corollary \ref{corollary_20_3}, the map $Y\defeq\Spa(B)\stackrel{\Spa(b)}{\to}\Omega$ where $b$ is the universal element is a homeomorphism sends following $\Rat_Y$ with $(\Rat_Y)_\Omega \defeq\{\Theta\in\Rat_X\sep \Theta\subseteq\Omega\}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
We denote $\beta = \Spa(b)$. By the assumption $\beta(Y)\subseteq \Omega$ hence the question is well-defined.
Let $v\in\Spa(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$ be a valuation since $v\in\Omega$, under the homeomorphism in Remark \ref{fact_6_3}, we lift $v_{f_0}\in\Cont(A_{f_0})$. 
Then by precomposing the evaluation map,
\begin{equation*}
    A^\triangleleft[X_1,\cdots,X_m]\ni P\stackrel{\ev}{\mapsto}P\left({\frac {f_1} {f_0}},\cdots{\frac {f_m} {f_0}}\right)\in A^\triangleleft_{f_0},
\end{equation*}
we obtain a lift $v_1\in\Spa(A^\triangleleft[X_1,\cdots,X_m],A^+[X_1,\cdots,X_m])$.
\begin{align*}
    v_1(P) &= v_{f_0}\left(\sum_{\alpha\in\N^m}p_\alpha\prod_{i=1}^m\left({\frac {f_i}{f_0}}\right)^{\alpha_i}\right),\\
    &\leq \max_{\alpha\in\N^m}v_0\left(p_\alpha\prod_{i=1}^m\left({\frac {f_i} {f_0}}\right)^{\alpha_i}\right),\\
    & \leq \max_{\alpha\in\N^m}v(p_\alpha)\prod_{i=1}^m\left({\frac {v(f_i)} {v(f_0)}}\right)^{\alpha_i},\\
    &\leq \max_{\alpha\in\N^m}v(p_\alpha) \tag{b}
\end{align*}
Hence for any $[0,\gamma)\subseteq\Gamma\cup\{0\}$ open, $K_{v\leq \gamma}[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ is open and the image under $v_1$ is contained in $[0,\gamma)$, hence $v_1\in\Spa(A^\triangleleft[X_1,\cdots,X_n],A^+[X_1,\cdots,X_n])$.
\par By Proposition \ref{proposition_21_4}, there is a unique continuous extension,
\begin{equation*}
    \widehat{v_1}:A^\triangleleft\langle X_1,\cdots,X_m\rangle\stackrel{r}{\to}\Gamma\cup\{0\}.
\end{equation*}
By construction $\widehat{v_1}$ annhilates $I$ since $\widehat{v_1}(f_0X_i-f_i) = 0$ and $\widehat{v_1}$ is continuous. %current Current
Furthermore, as $\widehat{v_1} = v(f_0) \not = 0$, hence this also kills $J$. Again using the identification in Remark \ref{fact_6_3}, we obtain $w:B^\triangleleft = (A^\triangleleft\langle X_1,\cdots,X_m\rangle/J)_{f_0}\to\Gamma\cup\{0\}$.
As $v_1\in\Spa(A^\triangleleft[X_1,\cdots,X_m],A^+[X_1,\cdots,X_m])$, using the continuity, 
\begin{equation*}
    A^+\langle X_1,\cdots,X_m\rangle\subseteq K_{w\leq 1}.
\end{equation*}
By Proposition \ref{fact_6_5}, $K_{w\leq 1}$ is integrally closed. Since $B^+$ is the integral closure of $A^+\langle X_1,\cdots,X_m\rangle$, $B^+\subseteq K_{w\leq 1}$. Hence $\beta(w) = v$.
\par Suppose we obtained another valuation $\widehat{v}_1'$ of $A^\triangleleft\langle X_1,\cdots,X_m\rangle$ from $v_1$.
Let $P\in A^\triangleleft[X_1,\cdots,X_m]$ be of degree $d$, then set 
\begin{equation*}
    Q(X_0,\cdots,X_m) \defeq X_0^dP\left({\frac {X_1} {X_0}},\cdots,{\frac {X_m} {X_0}}\right).
\end{equation*}
Set $q\defeq (f_0,\cdots,f_m)$, then $f_0^dP - q\in I$ since $Q \equiv q \mod{I}$. Since $\widehat{v}_1'$ kills $I$, hence 
\begin{equation*}
    \widehat{v}_1'(f_0)^d\widehat{v}_1'(P) = \widehat{v}_1'(q).
\end{equation*}
However, $\widehat{v}_1'(f_0) = v_{f_0}(f_0)\not=0$ and $\widehat{v}_1'(q) = v_{f_0}(q)$. Hence, $\widehat{v}_1'(P)$ depends solely on $v_{f_0}$ therefore it must coincide with $\widehat{v}_1$ on a dense subset. Using the continuity, we have shown the uniqueness of the extension.
All the other extensions were obtained from homeomorphisms, hence showing the injectivity.
\par It remains to show that $\beta$ is a homeomorphism. To do so, it is enough to show that $\beta$ is an open map. Consider a rational open $\Theta=\cR_Y(g_0|g_1,\cdots,g_k)$ where $\langle g_0,\cdots,g_k\rangle_B$ generates an open ideal.
Using Proposition \ref{proposition_18_3} together with the density of $A_{f_0}^\triangleleft$ in $B^\triangleleft$ by Corollary \ref{corollary_20_3}.b, there is an open subset $U\subseteq B^\triangleleft$ such that $g_i-b(r_i)\in U$ for each $i=0,\cdots,k$ and 
\begin{equation*}
\langle g_0,\cdots,g_k\rangle = \langle b(r_0),\cdots,b(r_k)\rangle,
\end{equation*}
hence open. Furthermore, as $f_0$ is a unit in $B^\triangleleft$, we may assume that $g_i = b(\alpha_i)$ for some $\alpha_i = f_0^dr_i\in A^\triangleleft$ for each $i=0,\cdots,k$ and fixed $d\geq0$. As $f^d_0U$ is an open subset, $V=b^{-1}(f_0^dU)$ is also open. Since $A^\triangleleft$ is Huber, by Proposition \ref{fact_17_10}, there is an finitely generated open ideal whose generators are topologically nilpotent elements $\{s_1,\cdots,s_l\}$. 
We may choose these elements small enough so that,
\begin{equation*}
    \langle s_1,\cdots,s_l\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}\subseteq V.
\end{equation*}
We extend $\alpha_i$ by those in order to obtain an open ideal $\langle \alpha_0,\cdots,\alpha_r\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}$.
\par Let $y\in Y$ and $x=\beta(y)$, then 
\begin{equation*}
    y(b(\alpha_i)) = x(\alpha_i).
\end{equation*}
As we can take the extending elements small enough, we conclude,
\begin{equation*}
    y\in\Theta\iff x\in\cR_X(\alpha_0|\alpha_1,\cdots,\alpha_r).
\end{equation*}
Hence,
\begin{equation*}
    \beta(\Theta) = \Omega\cap \cR_X(\alpha_0|\alpha_1,\cdots,\alpha_r)
\end{equation*}
which is open. Hence we have shown $\beta$ is a continuous open bijection hence a homeomorphism.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    In the setting of Corollary \ref{corollary_20_3}, let $\Theta\in\Rat_Y,C = (\Ouv_Y(\Theta),\Ouv_Y^+(\Theta))$. Set $\Theta_X\defeq \beta(\Theta)$ and take $c\in\mathcal{F}_\Theta(C)$ the universal element. The composition $A\stackrel{b}{\to}B\stackrel{c}{\to}C$ is an element of $\cF_{\Theta_X}$ which is a universal element induces an isomorphism, %has the universal property for that functor. The morphism,
        \begin{equation*}
            (\Ouv_X(\Theta_X),\Ouv_{X}^+(\Theta_X))\to(\Ouv_Y(\Theta),\Ouv_Y^+(\Theta))=C.
        \end{equation*}
        \label{theorem_21_2}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem \ref{theorem_21_1}, we have
\begin{equation*}
    \Theta_X = \beta(\Theta) = \Omega\cap\cR_X(\varphi_0|\varphi_1,\cdots,\varphi_m) = \cR_X(f_0\varphi_0|\text{ all other }f_i\varphi_j)
\end{equation*}
The topological claim then follows. If $\Theta_X = \beta(\Theta)$, one checks that 
\begin{align*}
    \mathcal{F}_\Theta(C)\ni(B\stackrel{c}{\to}C)&\rightarrow (A\stackrel{b}{\to}B\stackrel{c}{\to}C)\in\mathcal{F}_{\Theta_X}(C),
    %B\stackrel{v}{\to}C&\Rightarrow A\stackrel{\beta}{\to}B\stackrel{r}{\to}C
\end{align*}
is bijective usig the definition and the bijectivity of $\beta$. Thus the composition of structure presheaves also holds.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}
    \label{corollary_22_1}
    \begin{equation*}
        \Spa(A)\cong \Spa(\widehat{A})
    \end{equation*}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    Note that $\Spa(A) = \cR_{\Spa(A)}(1|)$. Hence the corresponding ring $B$ is just the completion of $A$.
\end{proof}

\section{Acyclicity proofs}

The Acyclicity proofs usually consider a special kinds of Čech cohomology to show that $\cO_X$ extends from $\Rat_X$ to $\Ouv_X$. Acyclicity will typically follow using the following statements.
\begin{proposition*}[Grothendieck's acyclicity lemma]
    Let $\mathscr{A}\stackrel{F}{\to}\mathscr{B}$ be a left exact functor between abelian categories where $\mathscr{A},\mathscr{B}$ contain sufficineltly many injective objects. Let $\mathscr{X}$ be a class of objects of $\mathscr{A}$
    such that 
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item Every direct summand of (in particular, every object isomorphic to) an element of $\mathscr{X}$ is in $\mathscr{X}$.
        \item Every objet $A$ of $\mathscr{A}$ has a monomorphism $A\to X$ with $X\in\mathscr{X}$.
        \item If 
        \begin{equation*}
            0\to X'\to X\to X''\to 0,
        \end{equation*}
         is a short exact sequence in $\mathscr{A}$ such that $X',X\in\mathscr{X}$, then $X'\in\mathscr{X}$ and 
        \begin{equation*}
            0\to FX'\to FX\to FX''\to 0.
        \end{equation*}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition*}
\begin{proof}
    See Grothendieck Tohoku Paper Lemma 3.3.1.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary*}
    All injective objects of $\sA$ belongs to $\sX$ and furthermore $X\in\sX$ and $p>0$, we have $R^pFX = 0$.
\end{corollary*}

\subsection{Acyclicity Criteria for General Sheaves on $\Spa A$}

In this subsection, we denote $X=\Spa A$ where $A$ is a Huber pair.

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_23_1}
    Let $x\in X$ be such that $\cGamma_x = \Gamma_x$ and $U$ be an open subset of $X$ such that $x\in U$. Then there is $m\in\N$ and $(f_i)_{i=0}^m\in(A^\triangleleft)^{m+1}$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \langle f_0,\cdots,f_m\rangle_{A^\triangleleft} = A^\triangleleft
    \end{equation*}
    and 
    \begin{equation*}
        x\in\cR_X(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_m)\subseteq U.
    \end{equation*}
    In particular, Proposition \ref{fact_15_11}, this holds when $x$ is closed.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Since rational open subsets form a topology base, there are $(\varphi_i)_{i=0}^m$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        x\in\cR_X(\varphi_0|\varphi_1,\cdots,\varphi_m)\subseteq U.
    \end{equation*}
    If $a\in A$ is such that $\ab{a}_x\not=0$, then this implies
    \begin{equation*}
        x\in\cR(a\varphi_0|a\varphi_1,\cdots,a\varphi_m)\subseteq\cR(\varphi_0|\varphi_1,\cdots,\varphi_m)\subseteq U.
    \end{equation*} 
    As $\ab{\varphi_0}_x\in\Gamma_x = \cGamma_x$, such $a$ can be chosen so that
    \begin{equation*}
        \ab{\varphi_0a}_x\geq 1.
    \end{equation*}
    Then 
    \begin{equation*}
        x\in\cR(a\varphi_0|a\varphi_1,\cdots,a\varphi_m,1)\subseteq\cR(a\varphi_0|a\varphi_1,\cdots,a\varphi_m)\subseteq U.
    \end{equation*}
    Obvisouly,
    \begin{equation*}
        \langle a\varphi_0,\cdots,a\varphi_m,1\rangle_{A^\triangleleft} = A^\triangleleft.
    \end{equation*}
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}$\:$
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $A$ being Tate or analytic is also sufficient to the above assertion for arbitrary $x$.
        \item Thus $\mathcal{O}_X(\cR(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_m))$ can be bounded by the ? Then 3b) (where ? $f_0$ is not necessary).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}$\:$
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item A rational covering of $X$ is a covering $X = \bigcup_{i=1}^m\cR_X(f_i|f_1,\cdots,f_m)$ such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \langle f_1,\cdots,f_m\rangle_{A^\triangleleft} = A^\triangleleft.
        \end{equation*}
        \item The rational sieve defined by such $(f_i)_{i=1}^m$ is 
        \begin{equation*}
            [\mathcal{R}(f_i|f_1,\cdots,f_m)\sep i=1,\cdots,m].
            %\{U\in\Ouv_X\sep U\subseteq\cR(f_i|f_1,\cdots,f_m)\text{ for some $i\in\{1,\cdots,m\}$}\}.
        \end{equation*}
        %i.e. the sieve generated by this covering.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_23_2}
    $\:$
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Every open covering $\U$ of $X$ has a refinement which is a rational covering.
        \item Every coering sieve (i.e. $X=\bigcup_{U\in\sS}U$) of $X$ contains a rational sieve.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Let $\sS$ be a covering sieve of $X$. By Proposition \ref{proposition_23_1}, for $x\in X$, we have $U_x\in\sS$ such that $x\in U_x$ and $(f_{x,i})_{i=0,\cdots,m_x}$ with 
    \begin{equation*}
        \langle f_{x,0},\cdots,f_{x,m_x}\rangle_{A^\triangleleft} = A^\triangleleft
    \end{equation*}
    such that $x\in\cR(f_{x,0}|f_{x,1},\cdots,f_{x,m_x})\subseteq U_x$. Then 
    \begin{equation*}
        V = \bigcup_{x\in X_{\mathrm{cl}}}\cR_X(f_{x,0}|f_{x,1},\cdots,f_{x,m_x}).
    \end{equation*} 
    Looking at the right hand side $V$ contains all closed points. If $V\subsetneqq X$, then $X\backslash V$ would be a non-empty closed subset without closed points, a contradiction by Proposition \ref{proposition_1_2_7}.
    \par As $X$ is spectral, it is quasi-compact, hence there are finitely many closed points $(x_i)_{i=1}^n$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        X = \bigcup_{i=1}^n\cR(f_{x_i,0}|f_{x_i,1},\cdots,f_{x_i,m_i}).
    \end{equation*}
    We denote $f_{i_j} = f_{x_i,j},n_i = m_{x_i},U_i = \cR(f_{i,0}|f_{i,1},\cdots,f_{i,m_i})$. Let $J$ be the set of functions defined on $\{1,\cdots,n\}$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
       \forall \iota\in J, 0\leq \iota(j)\leq m_j,
    \end{equation*}
    and let $J_0\subseteq J$ be the subset set of all $\iota\in J$ such that there is $j\in\{1,\cdots,n\}$ with $\iota(j)=0$. For all $\iota\in J$, let 
    \begin{equation*}
        f_\iota = \prod_{j=1}^nf_{j,\iota(j)}.
    \end{equation*}
    Clearly, 
    \begin{equation*}
        \langle f_\iota\sep \iota\in J\rangle_{A^\triangleleft} = \prod_{j=1}^n\langle f_{j,0},\cdots,f_{j,m}\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}=A^\triangleleft
    \end{equation*} 
    by the assumption. Note that 
    \begin{enumerate}[i.]
        %\item $X = \bigcup_{i=1}^n\cR_X(f_{j,0}|f_{j,1},\cdots,f_{j,m_j}) = \bigcup_{j=1}^m U_j \in\sS$.
        \item $\langle f_{j,0},\cdots,f_{j,m_j}\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}=A^\triangleleft$.
        % \item $\max_{\iota\in J}\ab{f_\iota}_x = \max_{\iota\in J_0}\ab{f_\iota(x)}$ when $x\in X$. Note that when $x\in U_k$ and $\iota(j) = \begin{cases}
        %     \iota(j),j\not=k,\\
        %     0,j=k.
        % \end{cases}$ then $\iota'\in J_0$ and $\ab{f_\iota}_x\leq \ab{f_{\iota'}}_x$.
        \item Since for eveyr $x\in X$, there is $j$ such that $x\in U_j$, we can improve any $f_{j,\iota(j)}$ by replacing it to $f_{j,0}$ hence $\max_{\iota\in J}\ab{f_\iota}_x = \max_{\iota\in J_0}\ab{f_\iota}_x$.
        \item Using Theorem \ref{theorem_17_2} twice, to $\langle f_\iota\sep \iota\in J\rangle$ and $\langle f_\iota\sep \iota\in J_0\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}$. We see that the latter also generates $A^\triangleleft$.% (Follows from b), c) and Theorem \ref{theorem_17_2}).
    \end{enumerate}
    Then the $(f_\iota)_{\iota\in J_0}$ defines a rational covering. We will show that the rational sieve generated by this is contained in $\sS$. To prove this claim, it suffices to consider,
    \begin{equation*}
        \Omega_\lambda = \cR(f_\lambda|\text{ all $f_\iota$ with $\iota\in J_0$}).
    \end{equation*}
    with $\lambda\in J_0$ and to show that $\Omega_\lambda\in\sS$, it suffices to find $j\in\{1,\cdots,n\}$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \Omega_\lambda\subseteq U_j.
    \end{equation*}
    We claim that this holds when $\lambda(j) = 0$. %(by Definition such $j$ exists for $\lambda\in J_0$).
    Indeed, let $x\in\Omega_\lambda$ then 
    \begin{equation*}
        \ab{f_\lambda}_x = \prod_{k=1}^n\ab{f_{k,\lambda(k)}}\not=0.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence $0\not=\ab{f_{j,\lambda(j)}}_x = \ab{f_{j,0}}_x$.
    % \begin{equation*}
    %     0\not=\ab{f_{j,\lambda(j)}}_x = \ab{f_{j,0}}_x.
    % \end{equation*}
    If $x\not\in U_j$, there would be $i\in \{1,\cdots,m_j\}$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \ab{f_{j,i}}_x>\ab{f_{j,0}}.
    \end{equation*}
    Let 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lambda'(k) = \begin{cases}
            \lambda(k),\quad k\not=j,\\
            i,\quad k=j.
        \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    Then 
    \begin{equation*}
        \ab{f_{\lambda'}(x)} = \ab{f_{j,i}(x)}\prod_{k\not=j}\ab{f_{k,\lambda(k)}}_x>\ab{f_{j,0}}_x\prod_{k\not=j}\ab{f_{k,\lambda(k)}}_x = \ab{f_\lambda(x)}
    \end{equation*}
    %For the inequality in the middle use that this is not $0$ as they are factors of $\ab{f_\lambda}_x\not=0$.
    Hence there is $\theta\in J_0$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
    \ab{f_\theta(x)}\geq \ab{f_{\lambda'}}_x>\ab{f_\lambda(x)},
    \end{equation*}
    contradicting $x\in\cR(f_\lambda|\text{ all $f_\iota$ for $\iota\in J_0$})\subseteq\cR(f_\lambda|f_\theta)$.
\end{proof}

%7/17

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_24_3}
    Let $A$ be a Huber pair and $X=\Spa A$ and $\mathcal{G}$ be a presheaf of abelian groups on $\Rat_X$ such that for every $\Omega\in\Rat_X$ and every rational covering $\mathcal{U}$ of $\Omega$, the map,
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{G}(\Omega)\to \check{H}^0(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{G})
    \end{equation*}
    is bijective. Then 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{G}\to(\underline{\Sheaf}(\mathcal{G}))_{\Rat_X}
    \end{equation*}
    is an isomorphism in the category of presheaves on $\Rat_X$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}
    Let $X=\Spa A$ where $A$ is a Huber pair. Set $\mathscr{X}$ to be a class of sheaves of abelian groups such that 
    \begin{equation*}
    \mathscr{X} = \left\{\cF\in\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Ab}}(X)\:\bigg{\vert}\:\substack{\forall p>0, \forall \Omega\in\Rat_X, \\\text{ and $\mathcal{U}$ rational covering of $\Omega$} \check{H}^p(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{F}) = 0}\right\},
    \end{equation*} %$\mathcal{F}$ of abelian groups on $X$ such that 
    % \begin{equation}
    %     \check{H}^p(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{F}) = 0
    % \end{equation}
    % where $p>0$ and $\mathcal{U}$ is a rational covering of $\Omega\in\Rat_X$, together with the functor
    together with a left exact functor
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{F}\mapsto\mathcal{F}(\Omega)
    \end{equation*}
    where $\Omega\in\Rat_X$ satisfies the assumption of Grothendieck's acyclicity lemma. Hence due to corollary of it, all injective sheaves are in $\mathscr{X}$. In particular, $\Omega\in\Rat_X,\mathcal{F}\in\mathscr{X}$ and $p>0$ then $H^p(\Omega,\mathcal{F})=0$.
\end{proposition}

%We have somewhat more convenient acyclicity criterion in the Tate case.

Under the assumption of $A$ being Tate, we have more convenient acyclicity criterion.

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_24_2}
    Let $X=\Spa A$ where $(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$ is a Huber pair. We inductively define the property of a sieve $\sS$ in $\Omega\in\Rat_X$ to have a Laurent order at most $o$ as follows.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item A sieve has Laurent order $0$ if it is the all sieve.
        \item If $o>0$ and Laurent order $\leq o-1$ has already been defined then $\sS$ has Laurent order $\leq o$ if there is $g\in \cO_X(\Omega)$ such that $\sS|_{\cR_\Omega(g|1)}$ and $\sS|_{\cR_\Omega(1|g)}$ both have Laurent order $\leq o-1$. 
    \end{enumerate}
    The Laurent order of $\sS$ is the smallest $o\in N$ such that $\sS$ has Laurent order $\leq o$ if no such $o$ exists then its Laurent order is $\infty$. We denote $o_L(\sS)$ to be the Laurent order of $\sS$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_24_1}Let $\sS$ be a sieve on $\Omega$. Then the following statements hold.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $\sS\subseteq\mathcal{T}\Rightarrow o_L(\mathcal{T})\leq o_L(\sS)$. 
        \item $\Theta\subseteq\Omega\Rightarrow o_{L,\Theta}(\sS|_\Theta)\leq o_{L,\Omega}(\sS)$.
        \item $o_L(\sS)<\infty\Rightarrow \sS$ is a covering sieve.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

%The following lemma does not yet need the Tate assumption.

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_24_1}
    Let $G=\{g_i\sep 1\leq i\leq n\}$ be a non-empty finite subset of $\cO_X(\Omega)^\times$. %$(A^{\triangleleft})\times$. 
    Then the rational sieve defined by $G$ has finite Laurent order.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    We write $g_i\preceq g_j$ if $\Omega=\cR(g_j|g_i)$. Obviously, this is a partial order with allowed equivalence. We call $(i,j)$ in order if at least one of $g_i\preceq g_j$ or $g_j\preceq g_i$ holds. 
    We prove the claim by induction on the number of pairs not in order. 
    \par If all pairs are in order then without loss of generality by renumbering $G$, we can assume $g_1\preceq \cdots\preceq g_m$. Thus, all $g_i\preceq g_m$. In this case, observe that 
    \begin{equation*}
    \Omega = \cR_\Omega(g_m|g_0,\cdots,g_{m-1}).
    \end{equation*}
    Hence the rational sieve generated by the rational cover of $G$ contains $\Omega$ hence is the all sieve. Therefore by definition, it has Laurent order $0$.
    \par Suppose $(i,j)$ is not in order and the assertion shown for a smaller number of pairs not in order. Let $q ={\frac {g_i} {g_j}}$ and $\Theta_1 = \cR_\Omega(q|1)$ and $\Theta_2=\cR_\Omega(1|q)$. Then the pairs in order stay in order after restricting to $\Theta_i$ as they are subsets of $\Omega$. Furthermore, $g_j|_{\Theta_1}\preceq g_i|_{\Theta_1}$ and $g_i|_{\Theta_2}\preceq g_j|_{\Theta2}$.
    Thus $(i,j)$ becomes in order for $G_k = \{g_i|_{\Theta_k}\sep 1\leq i\leq n\}$ where $k=1,2$. By the induction assumption and Definition \ref{definition_24_2}, the rational sieve defined by $G$ has finite Laurent order.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_24_2}
    Let $A=(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$ be a Huber pair such that $A^\triangleleft$ is Tate and there is $(f_i)_{i=0}^m\in A^{m+1}$ with $\langle f_0,\cdots,f_m\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}=A^\triangleleft$. Then we can take $\varepsilon\in (A^{\triangleleft})^\times\cap (A^{\triangleleft})^\dcirc$ satisfying
    \begin{equation*}
        \forall x\in X,\ab{\varepsilon}_x \leq\max_{0\leq i\leq n}\ab{f_i}_x.\label{eq:lemma_24_2_3}
    \end{equation*}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Let $s\in (A^{\triangleleft})^\dcirc\cap (A^{\triangleleft})^\times$. By assumption, there are $(\lambda_i)_{i=0,\cdots,m}\in (A^\triangleleft)^{m+1}$ giving
    \begin{equation*}
        \sum_{i=0}^m \lambda_i f_i = 1.
    \end{equation*}
    Since $A^+$ is an open subring and $\lim_{j\to\infty} s^j\lambda_i = 0$, there is $j\in\N$ such that for each $i=0,\cdots,m$, $\lambda_is^j\in A^+$. Then one can set $\varepsilon = s^j$. Indeed,
    \begin{equation*}
        \ab{\varepsilon}_x =\left\vert\sum_{i=0}^m (s^j\lambda_i)f_i\right\vert_x\leq\max_{0\leq i\leq m}\ab{\lambda_is^j}_x\ab{f_i}_x\leq \max_{0\leq i\leq m}\ab{f_i}_x.
    \end{equation*}
    % since $\ab{\lambda_is^j}\leq 1$ since $x\in\Spa A$ and $\lambda_i s^j\in A^+$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_24_5}
    In the situation of Definition \ref{definition_24_2}, assume $A$ (i.e $A^\triangleleft$) to be Tate. Then a sieve $\sS$ on $\Omega\in\Rat_X$ covers $\Omega$ if and only if $o_L(\sS)<\infty$. 
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    The proof will work by showing that every rational sieve has finite $o_L$ which proves the claim by Proposition \ref{proposition_23_2} and \ref{fact_24_1} a.
    \par It is sufficent to show that every rational sieve has finite Laurent order. Let $\sS$ be the rational sieve defined by the finite subset $G$ of $\cO_X(\Omega)$. We use induction on the number of elements of $G$ which are not in $\mathcal{O}_X^\times(\Omega)$.
    If this number is $0$, Lemma \ref{lemma_24_1} can be applied. Suppose $g_1\not\in\mathcal{O}_X^\times(\Omega)$ and the assertion shown for a smaller number of non-elements.
    Since $A$ is Tate, there is $\delta\in\mathcal{O}_X^\times(\Omega)$ such that $\ab{\delta}_\omega <\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\ab{g_i}_\omega$ holds for all $\omega\in\Omega$. Indeed, from the proof of Lemma \ref{lemma_24_2} shows the strict inequality can be attained. Consider 
    \begin{equation*}
        \Theta_1 = \cR_\Omega(g_1|\delta)=\cR\left({\frac {g_1} \delta}\bigg{\vert}1\right),\Theta_2 = \cR_\Omega(\delta|g_1) = \cR\left(1\bigg{\vert}{\frac {g_1} \delta}\right).
    \end{equation*}
    On $\Theta_1,g_1$ becomes a unit as $\ab{g_1}_\theta\geq\ab{\delta}_\theta\not=0$ when $\theta\in\Theta_1$ hence the induction assumption applies to $G|_{\Theta_1}$. 
    On the other hand for $\theta_2\in\Theta_2$, 
    \begin{equation*}
        \ab{g_1}_{\theta_2}\leq \ab{\delta}_{\theta_2}<\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\ab{g_i}_{\theta_2}.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, we obtain,
    \begin{equation*}
        \cR_{\Theta_2}(g_1|g_2,\cdots,g_m) = \emptyset.
    \end{equation*}
    Furthermore, the inequality shows,
    \begin{equation*}
        \forall 2\leq i\leq m, \cR_{\Theta_2}(g_i|g_1,\cdots,g_m) = \cR_{\Theta_2}(g_i|g_2,\cdots,g_m).
    \end{equation*}
    Applying Theorem \ref{theorem_17_2}.d, and that $g_2|_{\Theta_2},\cdots g_m|_{\Theta_2}$ have no common zero on $\Theta_2$, these also define a rational covering, hence the induction hypothesis can be applied.
    % But the induction assumption also applies to $G|_\Theta$ since
    % \begin{equation*}
    %     \cR_{\Theta_2}(g_1|g_2,\cdots,g_m)=\emptyset 
    % \end{equation*}
    % as for $\vartheta\in\Theta_2$, $\ab{g_1}_\vartheta\leq\ab{\delta}_\vartheta<\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\ab{g_i}_\vartheta$. And,
    % \begin{equation*}
    %     \cR_{\Theta_2}(g_i|g_1,g_2,\cdots,g_m) = \cR(g_i|g_2,\cdots,g_m)
    % \end{equation*}
    % as for $\vartheta\in\Theta_2$, 
    % \begin{equation}
    %     \ab{g_1}_\vartheta\leq\ab{\delta}_\vartheta<\max_{2\leq i\leq m}\ab{g_i}_\vartheta.\label{eq:proposition_24_5_star}
    % \end{equation}
    % \par Hence $g_1|_{\Theta_2}$ can simply be removed from $G_{\Theta_2}$ without affection the rational sieve by \eqref{eq:proposition_24_5_star} it follows that $g_2|_{\Theta_2},\cdots,g_m|_{\Theta_2}$ have no common zero in $\Theta_2$, hence define a rational sieve.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_24_6}
    Let $X=\Spa A$ where $A$ is Tate, $\mathcal{G}$ be a presheaf of abelian groups on $\Rat_X$ such that for all $\Omega\in\Rat_X$ and all $g\in\mathcal{O}_X(\Omega)$, the sequence,
%     \begin{equation}
%         \begin{tikzcd}
% 0 \arrow[r] & \mathcal{G}(\Omega) \arrow[r, "{r\mapsto(r|_{\Omega_1},r|_{\Omega_2})}"] & \mathcal{G}(\Omega_1)\oplus\mathcal{G}(\Omega_2) \arrow[r, "{(r_1,r_2)\mapsto(r_1|_{\Omega_{12}}-r_2|_{\Omega_{12}})}"] & \mathcal{G}(\Omega_{12}) \arrow[r] & 0
% \end{tikzcd}
%     \end{equation}
    \begin{equation*}
        0\to\mathcal{G}(\Omega)\stackrel{\substack{\mathmakebox[0pt][c]{r\mapsto(r|_{\Omega_1},r|_{\Omega_2})}\\\:}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{G}(\Omega_1)\oplus\mathcal{G}(\Omega_2)\stackrel{\substack{\mathmakebox[0pt][c]{(r_1,r_2)\mapsto(r_1|_{\Omega_{12}}-r_2|_{\Omega_{12}})}\\\:}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{G}(\Omega_{12})\to0.
    \end{equation*}
    is exact where $\Omega_1 = \cR_\Omega(g|1),\Omega_2 = \cR_\Omega(1|g)$ and $\Omega_{12} = \Omega_1\cap \Omega_2$. 
    \par Then $\mathcal{G}\to(\underline{\Sheaf}(\mathcal{G}))|_{\Rat_X}$ is an isomorphism in the category of pressheaves of abelian groups on $X$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_24_7}
    Let $X=\Spa A$ where the Huber pair $A$ is Tate. %Then the assumption in Grothendieck's acyclicity lemma holds for % and of the functor $\mathcal{F}\to \mathcal{F}(\Omega)$ and $\mathscr{X}$ the class of sheaves $\mathcal{F}$ on $X$ such that 
    Let $\mathscr{X}$ be a subclass of $\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Ab}}(X)$ containing any $\cF\in\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Ab}}(X)$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        o\to\mathcal{F}(\Omega)\stackrel{\substack{\mathmakebox[0pt][c]{f\mapsto(f|_{\Omega_1},f|_{\Omega_2})}\\\:}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{F}(\Omega_1)\oplus\mathcal{F}(\Omega_2)\stackrel{\substack{\mathmakebox[0pt][c]{(f_1,f_2)\mapsto(f_1|_{\Omega_12}-f_2|_{\Omega_{12}})}\\\:}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{F}(\Omega_{12})\to 0
    \end{equation*}
    is exact for all $\Omega\in\Rat_X$ and all $g\in\mathcal{O}_X(\Omega)$ where $\Omega_1,\Omega_2$ and $\Omega_{12}$ are as in Proposition \ref{proposition_24_6}. Then the assumption in Grothendieck's acyclicity lemma holds for $\mathscr{X}$ and $\mathscr{A}=\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Ab}}(X)$ with the functor $\cF\mapsto \cF(\Omega)$ for any $\Omega\in\Rat_X$.
\end{proposition}
